Consider the monad
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}
(carried by
\mathcal{Q}^{(\Sigma_{\tau}\times{I\mspace{-1mu}d}+\{\checkmark\})}
) obtained by equipping the quantale monad with labelled, unobservable moves and accepting as described above. We will refer to this monad as the
quantalic automata monad
since coalgebras for its endofunctor
\mathcal{Q}^{(\Sigma_{\tau}\times{I\mspace{-1mu}d})}
are quantalic automata with
\varepsilon
-moves (we adopt the same notation used for
\varepsilon
-NA and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}
). Composition in the Kleisli category for this monad assigns to every
f\colon X\to Y
and
g\colon Y\to Z
the composite map:

(g\circ f)(x)(w)=\begin{cases}\bigvee\left\{f(x)(\checkmark),f(x)(\tau,y)\cdot g(y)(\checkmark)\,\middle|\,y\in Y\right\}&\text{if }w=\checkmark\\
\bigvee\left\{f(x)(\sigma_{1},y)\cdot g(y)(\sigma_{2},z)\,\middle|\,\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}=\{\sigma,\tau\}\text{ and }y\in Y\right\}&\text{if }w=(\sigma,z)\end{cases}

The monad
{\mathcal{Q}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}
is equipped the left, right, and double strengths given below:

\displaystyle\mathit{lstr}_{X,Y}(x,\phi)(w)=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\phi(\checkmark)&\text{if }w=\checkmark\\
\phi(\sigma,y^{\prime})&\text{if }w=(\sigma,x,y^{\prime})\\
\perp&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathit{rstr}_{X,Y}(\psi,y)(w)=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\psi(\checkmark)&\text{if }w=\checkmark\\
\psi(\sigma,x^{\prime})&\text{if }w=(\sigma,x^{\prime},y)\\
\perp&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\mathit{dstr}_{X,Y}(\psi,\phi)(w)=

\displaystyle\begin{cases}\psi(\checkmark)\vee\phi(\checkmark)&\text{if }w=\checkmark\\
\bigvee\left\{\psi(\sigma_{1},x)\cdot\phi(\sigma_{2},y)\mid\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}=\{\sigma,\tau\}\right\}&\text{if }w=(\sigma,x,y)\end{cases}

The tensor of the resulting monoidal structure on
\textnormal{Kl}({\mathcal{Q}}^{\Sigma})
takes every pair of sets
X
and
Y
to their cartesian product
X\times Y
and every pair of morphisms
f_{1}\colon X_{1}\to Y_{1}
and
f_{2}\colon X_{2}\to Y_{2}
to the arrow given by the function:

(f_{1}{\mathchoice{{\hbox to12pt{\vbox to9.31pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 5.99998pt\lower-4.33333pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {
{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{1.0}{0.0}{0.0}{1.0}{-4.99998pt}{-2.5pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}

7.1.2.
Behavioural equivalences

Let
\alpha
be a timed quantalic system with carrier
X
and inputs from the alphabet
\Sigma_{\tau}
. We write
x\xrightarrow{t}\psi
for a timed step in
\alpha

i
.
e
.:

\displaystyle x\xrightarrow{t}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\psi=\alpha_{t}(x)\text{.}

We write
x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\psi
for a saturated timed step in
\alpha

i
.
e
.:

\displaystyle x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\psi=\lambda(\sigma,x^{\prime}).\bigvee\left\{\phi(\sigma,x^{\prime})\,\middle|\,x^{\prime}\in n<\omega\text{, }X\text{, and }x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}_{n}\phi\right\}

where the (functional) relation
{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}_{n}
is given by recursion on the number
n
of underlying steps as follows:

\displaystyle x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}_{0}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle t=0\text{ and }\psi(\sigma,x^{\prime})=\begin{cases}1&\text{if }\sigma=\tau\text{ and }x=x^{\prime}\\
\perp&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}_{n+1}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\psi=\lambda(\sigma,x^{\prime}).\bigvee\left\{\phi_{1}(\sigma_{1},x^{\prime\prime})\cdot\phi_{2}(\sigma_{2},x^{\prime})\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}x^{\prime\prime}\in X\text{, }\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}=\{\sigma,\tau\}\text{,}\\
x\xrightarrow{t^{\prime}}\phi_{1}\text{, and }x^{\prime\prime}{\mathrel{\to}^{t-t^{\prime}}}_{n}\phi_{2}\end{array}\right\}

For an equivalence relation
R
on the carrier
X
of
\alpha
, we write
\equiv_{R}
for its lifting to
\mathcal{Q}^{\Sigma_{\tau}\times X}
:

\displaystyle\psi\equiv_{R}\phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\forall C\in X/R\bigvee_{x\in C}\psi(x)=\bigvee_{x\in C}\phi(x)\text{.}

Definition 7.3
.

For a timed quantalic system
\alpha
and an equivalence relation
R
on its carrier:

•

R
is a
(strong) timed bisimulation
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}x^{\prime}
and
x\xrightarrow{t}\psi
implies that there are
x^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t}\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi\equiv_{R}\psi^{\prime}
;

•

R
is a
(strong) time-abstract bisimulation
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}x^{\prime}
and
x\xrightarrow{t}\psi
implies that there are
x^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi\equiv_{R}\psi^{\prime}
;

•

R
is a
weak timed bisimulation
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}x^{\prime}
and
x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\psi
implies that there are
x^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi\equiv_{R}\psi^{\prime}
;

•

R
is a
weak time-abstract bisimulation
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}x^{\prime}
and
x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\psi
implies that there are
x^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}^{t^{\prime}}}\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi\equiv_{R}\psi^{\prime}
;

Timed quantalic systems are modelled in the lax functors framework by putting
K
to be
\textnormal{Kl}({\mathcal{Q}}^{\Sigma})
. Notions of behavioural equivalence for TQSs defined above are all instances of
q
-bisimulation where behavioural morphisms are sourced from
Set
.

Proposition 7.4
.

For a timed quantalic system
\alpha
with functor model
\underline{\alpha}
and an equivalence relation
R
on the carrier of
\alpha
:

(1)

R
is a (strong) timed bisimulation for
\alpha
iff it is a
id_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(2)

R
is a (strong) time-abstract bisimulation for
\alpha
iff it is a
!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(3)

R
is a weak timed bisimulation for
\alpha
iff it is a
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{T}}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(4)

R
is a weak time-abstract bisimulation for
\alpha
iff it is a
!_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
;

7.1.3.
Language equivalences

Languages accepted by a timed quantalic systems are defined as in the non-deterministic case except that acceptance is no longer a boolean property but a weighted one
i
.
e
. functions from the set of all timed (resp. untimed) words to the set carrying the given quantale
\mathcal{Q}
. Let
\alpha
be a timed quantalic system with accepting moves and let
X
be its state space. Formally, the timed/untimed languages strongly/weakly accepted by a state
x_{0}
of
\alpha
are the functions:

\displaystyle\mathrm{tl}_{\alpha}(x_{0})(t_{0}\,\sigma_{1}\,t_{1}\,\sigma_{1}\dots t_{n})\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigvee\left\{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}(\sigma_{i},x_{i})\right)\cdot\psi_{n}(\checkmark)\,\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in X\text{ and}\\
x_{i}\xrightarrow{t_{i}}\psi_{i}\text{ for }i\leq n\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{utl}_{\alpha}(x_{0})(\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n})\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigvee\big\{\mathrm{tl}_{\alpha}(x_{0})(t_{0}\,\sigma_{1}\,t_{1}\dots t_{n})\,\big|\,t_{0},\dots,t_{n}\in\mathbb{T}\big\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{wtl}_{\alpha}(x_{0})(t_{0}\,\sigma_{1}\,t_{1}\,\sigma_{1}\dots t_{n})\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigvee\left\{\left(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}(\sigma_{i},x_{i})\right)\cdot\psi_{n}(\checkmark)\,\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\in X\text{ and}\\
x_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{t_{i}}}\psi_{i}\text{ for }i\leq n\end{array}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathrm{wutl}_{\alpha}(x_{0})(\sigma_{1}\ldots\sigma_{n})\triangleq

\displaystyle\bigvee\big\{\mathrm{wtl}_{\alpha}(x_{0})(t_{0}\,\sigma_{1}\,t_{1}\dots t_{n})\,\big|\,t_{0},\dots,t_{n}\in\mathbb{T}\big\}\text{.}

Definition 7.5
.

For a timed quantalic system
\alpha
and an equivalence relation
R
on its carrier:

•

R
is a
timed language equivalence
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}y
implies that
\mathrm{tl}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathrm{tl}_{\alpha}(y)
;

•

R
is a
time-abstract language equivalence
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}y
implies that
\mathrm{utl}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathrm{utl}_{\alpha}(y)
;

•

R
is a
weak timed language equivalence
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}y
implies that
\mathrm{wtl}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathrm{wtl}_{\alpha}(y)
;

•

R
is a
weak time-abstract language equivalence
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}y
implies that
\mathrm{wutl}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathrm{wutl}_{\alpha}(y)
.

Quantalic systems with accepting moves are modelled in the lax functors framework by putting
K
to be
\textnormal{Kl}({\mathcal{Q}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark})
. Notions of language equivalence for TQSs defined above are all instances of
q
-bisimulation where behavioural morphisms are sourced from
\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{Q}^{(-)})
.

Proposition 7.6
.

For a timed quantalic system
\alpha
with functor model
\underline{\alpha}
and an equivalence relation
R
on the carrier of
\alpha
:

(1)

R
is a timed language equivalence for
\alpha
iff it is a
id_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-language equivalence for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(2)

R
is a time-abstract language equivalence for
\alpha
iff it is a
!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}
-language equivalence for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(3)

R
is a weak timed language equivalence for
\alpha
iff it is a
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{T}}
-language equivalence for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(4)

R
is a weak time-abstract language equivalence for
\alpha
iff it is a
!_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-language equivalence for
\underline{\alpha}
;

It follows from
Propositions

7.4
,
7.6
,
4.12
and
4.14
that notions of bisimulation and language equivalence for TQSs given in
Definitions

7.3
and
7.5
are organised in the spectrum shown in
Figure

1
.

7.2.
Segala systems

Segala systems are systems whose computations express a combination of non-deterministic and probabilistic aspects. These systems are
Segala 1995
who pioneered their study
[
41
]
.

7.2.1.
Model

Setting aside for a moment inputs, acceptance, and other aspects typical of automata and focusing on the branching type of Segala systems, the computations expressed by these systems can be ascribed to the functor
\mathcal{PD}
where
\mathcal{D}
is the probability distribution monad
[
45
]
. Despite the fact that both functors carry monad structures, their composite does not fail to be a proper monad
[
17
,
48
]
. In order to compose single steps of these machines into sequences and computations, Segala introduced the notion of
combined step
where non-determinism is restricted to those subsets that are closed under convex combinations—hence the term
convex semantics
(of Segala systems). This semantics is captured by the convex combination monad
4
4

4

The convex combinations monad was first introduced in its full generality by
Jacobs 2008
in
[
17
]
to study trace semantics for combined possibilistic and probabilistic systems. Independently,
Brengos 2015b

[
7
]
and
Goncharov and Pattinson 2014

[
12
]
tweaked Jacobs’ construction slightly, so that the resulting monads are more suitable to model Segala systems and their weak bisimulations.
Jacobs 2008
’ monad,
Brengos 2015b
’ monad and
Goncharov and Pattinson 2014
’s monad identify Kleisli categories that are
DCpo
-enriched and whose hom-sets admit binary joins. For the purposes of this paper we take the convex combinations monad
{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}\colon\textnormal{Set}\to\textnormal{Set}
to be that considered in
[
7
, §8]
.

[
17
,
48
,
7
,
12
,
33
]
.

Convex set monad.

By
[0,\infty)
we denote the semiring
([0,\infty),+,0,\cdot,1)
of non-negative real numbers with ordinary addition and multiplication. By a
[0,\infty)
-
semimodule
we mean the commutative monoid with actions
[0,\infty)\times(-)\to(-)
satisfying axioms listed in
e
.
g
.
[
11
]
. For a set
X
and define
\mathcal{M}X
as the set of finitely supported weight functions:

\mathcal{M}X\triangleq\{\phi\colon X\to[0,\infty)\mid\supp(\phi)\text{ is finite}\}

where
\supp(\phi)
is the
support
of
\phi\colon X\to[0,\infty)

i
.
e
. the set
\{x\mid\phi(x)\neq 0\}
of elements assigned a non zero value by
\phi
.
We will often denote elements of
\mathcal{M}X
using the formal sum notation: for
\phi\in\mathcal{M}X
we write
\sum_{x}\phi(x)\cdot x
or, given
\supp(\phi)=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{n}\}
, simply
\sum_{i=1,\ldots,n}\phi(x_{i})\cdot x_{i}
. Any function
f\colon X\to Y
induces the action
\mathcal{M}f\colon\mathcal{M}X\to\mathcal{M}Y
defined as follows:

\mathcal{M}(f)(\phi)=\sum\phi(x)\cdot f(x)=\lambda y.\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)}\phi(x)

It is immediate to check that this data defines the (finite generalised) multiset functor.
The set
\mathcal{M}X
carries a monoid structure via pointwise operation of addition, and
[0,\infty)
-action via

(a\cdot\phi)(x)\triangleq a\cdot\phi(x)\text{,}

which turn
\mathcal{M}X
into a free semimodule over
X
(see
e
.
g
.
[
7
,
11
,
17
]
for details). For a non-empty subset
U\subseteq\mathcal{M}X
we define its convex closure as the set:

\overline{U}=\left\{a_{1}\cdot\phi_{1}+\ldots+a_{n}\cdot\phi_{n}\;\middle|\;\phi_{i}\in U,\ a_{i}\in[0,\infty)\text{ such that }\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}=1\right\}\text{.}

Call a subset
U\subseteq\mathcal{M}X

convex
provided
\overline{U}=U
and put

{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}X=\{U\subseteq\mathcal{M}X\mid U\text{ is convex and non-empty}\}\text{.}

Addition and
[0,\infty)
-actions are extended to convex sets in a pointwise manner: for
U,V\subseteq\mathcal{M}X
both convex and
a\in[0,\infty)
we write
U+V
for the set
\{\phi+\psi\mid\phi\in U,\psi\in V\}
and
a\cdot U
for the set
\{a\cdot\phi\mid\phi\in U\}
.

Every function
f\colon X\to Y
induces an action
{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}(f)\colon{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}X\to{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}Y
given on each convex subset
U
of semimodules on
X
as follows:

{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}(f)(U)=\{\mathcal{M}(f)(\phi)\mid\phi\in U\}\text{.}

The assignment
{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}\colon\textnormal{Set}\to\textnormal{Set}
is a functor which carries a monadic structure
[
7
]
whose multiplication and unit have as component at
X
the functions:

\mu_{X}(U)\triangleq\bigcup_{\phi\in U}\sum_{V\in{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}X}\{\phi(V)\cdot\psi\mid\psi\in V\}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\eta_{X}(x)\triangleq\{1\cdot x\}\text{.}

The convex combination monad is equipped with strength, costrength and double strength given on each component as:

\displaystyle\mathit{lstr}_{X,Y}(x,U)

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum\phi(y)\cdot(x,y)\,\middle|\,\phi\in U\right\}

\displaystyle\mathit{rstr}_{X,Y}(V,y)

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum\psi(x)\cdot(x,y)\,\middle|\,\psi\in V\right\}

\displaystyle\mathit{dstr}_{X,Y}(V,U)

\displaystyle=\left\{\sum(\psi(x)\cdot\phi(y))\cdot(x,y)\,\middle|\,\psi\in V,\phi\in U\right\}\text{.}

Composition in
\textnormal{Kl}({\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}})
is given, for each
f
and
g
with compatible domains and codomains as:

(g\circ f)(x)=\bigcup_{\phi\in f(x)}\sum_{y\in\supp(\phi)}\left\{\phi(y)\cdot\psi\mid\psi\in g(y)\right\}

where the underlying functions in
Set
have type
f\colon X\to{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}(Y)
,
g\colon Y\to{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}(Z)
, and
g\circ f

\textnormal{Kl}({\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}})
.

Unobservable and accepting moves.

Akin to
{\mathcal{P}}^{\Sigma}
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}
, we extend
{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}
with unobservable moves and acceptance via the general construction introduced in
[
7
]
and introduce the
Segala systems monad

{{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}
and the
Segala automata monad

{{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}
over the endofunctors
{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}(\Sigma_{\tau}\times{I\mspace{-1mu}d})
and
{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}(\Sigma_{\tau}\times{I\mspace{-1mu}d}+\{\checkmark\})
, respectively. Since we are interested in the Kleisli categories of these monads, we omit an explicit derivation of their structure and instead we describe directly
\textnormal{Kl}({{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma})
and
\textnormal{Kl}({{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark})
.
The Kleisli category
\textnormal{Kl}({{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma})
has sets as objects and maps
X\to{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}(\Sigma_{\tau}\times Y)
as morphisms from
X
to
Y
. For
f\colon X\to{{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}(Y)
and
g\colon Y\to{{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}(Z)
the composite
g\circ f\in\textnormal{Kl}({{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma})
is the map:

\displaystyle(g\circ f)(x)={}

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\phi\in f(x)}\left(\sum_{y\in Y}\{\phi(\tau,y)\cdot\psi\mid\psi\in g(y)\}+{}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\sum_{\sigma\in\Sigma,\,y\in Y}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(\sigma,y)\cdot r_{i}\cdot(\sigma,z_{i})\,\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}\sum_{j=1}^{n+m}r_{j}\cdot(\sigma_{j},z_{j})\in g(y)\text{ such that}\\
\sigma_{j}=\tau\iff j\leq n\end{array}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in Z,\sigma\in\Sigma_{\tau}\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{y\in Y,\,\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}=\{\sigma,\tau\}}\mspace{-12mu}\phi(\sigma_{1},y)\cdot\psi_{y}(\sigma_{2},z)\right)\cdot(\sigma,z)\,\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}\phi\in f(x),\\
\{\psi_{y}\}_{y\in\supp(\phi)},\\
\psi_{y}\in g(y)\end{array}\right\}\text{.}

The above Kleisli category (see
[
7
]
for a discussion) lets us consider Segala systems with unobservable moves as endomorphisms and allows their mutual composition. Likewise, the Kleisli category
\textnormal{Kl}({{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark})
has sets as objects and maps
X\to{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}(\Sigma_{\tau}\times Y+\{\checkmark\})
as morphisms from
X
to
Y
. For
f\colon X\to{{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}(Y)
and
g\colon Y\to{{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}(Z)
the composite
g\circ f\in\textnormal{Kl}({{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark})
is the map:

\displaystyle(g\circ f)(x)={}

\displaystyle\bigcup_{\phi\in f(x)}\left(\{\phi(\checkmark)\cdot\checkmark\}+\sum_{y\in Y}\{\phi(\tau,y)\cdot\psi\mid\psi\in g(y)\}+{}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\sum_{y\in Y,\,\sigma\in\Sigma}\left\{\sum_{i=1}^{n}\phi(\sigma,y)\cdot r_{i}\cdot(\sigma,z_{i})\,\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}\sum_{j=1}^{n+m}r_{j}\cdot(\sigma_{j},z_{j})\in g(y)\text{ such that}\\
\sigma_{i}=\tau\iff i\leq n\end{array}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle={}

\displaystyle\left\{\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}z\in Z,\ \sigma\in\Sigma_{\tau}\end{subarray}}\left(\sum_{y\in Y,\,\{\sigma_{1},\sigma_{2}\}=\{\sigma,\tau\}}\mspace{-12mu}\phi(\sigma_{1},y)\cdot\psi_{y}(\sigma_{2},z)\right)\cdot(\sigma,z)+{}\right.

\displaystyle\left.\left(\phi(\checkmark)+\sum_{y\in Y}\phi(\tau,y)\cdot\psi_{y}(\checkmark)\right)\cdot\checkmark\,\middle|\begin{array}[]{l}\phi\in f(x),\\
\{\psi_{y}\in g(y)\}_{y\in\supp(\phi)}\end{array}\right\}\text{.}

Akin to how endomorphisms in
\textnormal{Kl}({\mathcal{P}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark})
model non-deterministic automata, endomorphisms in
\textnormal{Kl}({{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark})
are a model for Segala automata since
{{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}
extends
{{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}
with acceptance mimicking the situation of
{\mathcal{P}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}
and
{\mathcal{P}}^{\Sigma}
.

7.2.2.
Behavioural equivalences

Let
\alpha
be a timed Segala system with carrier
X
and inputs from the alphabet
\Sigma_{\tau}
. We write
x\xrightarrow{t}\psi
for a timed step in
\alpha

i
.
e
.:

\displaystyle x\xrightarrow{t}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\psi\in\alpha_{t}(x)\text{.}

We write
x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\psi
for a saturated timed step in
\alpha

i
.
e
.:

\displaystyle x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\exists n\,x{\mathrel{\to}^{t,n}}\psi

where the relation
{\mathrel{\to}^{t,n}}
is given by recursion on the number
n
of underlying steps as follows:

\displaystyle x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}_{0}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle t=0\text{ and }\psi=1\cdot(\tau,x)\text{,}

\displaystyle x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}_{n+1}\psi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\exists\,x\xrightarrow{t^{\prime}}{\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{m}r_{i}\cdot(\sigma_{i},x^{\prime}_{i})},\ \forall\,i\in\{1,\dots,m\}\,

\displaystyle\exists\,x^{\prime}_{i}{\mathrel{\to}^{t-t^{\prime}}}_{n}{\textstyle\sum_{j=0}^{m_{i}}r^{\prime}_{i,j}\cdot(\sigma^{\prime}_{i,j},x^{\prime\prime}_{i,j})},\ \forall j\in\{1,\dots,m_{i}\}\,\exists\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i,j}\in\Sigma_{\tau}

\displaystyle\text{ s.t. }\{\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i,j},\tau\}=\{\sigma_{i},\sigma^{\prime}_{i,j}\}\,\,\text{ and }\psi={\textstyle\sum_{i=0}^{m}\sum_{j=0}^{m_{i}}\left(r_{i}\cdot r^{\prime}_{i,j}\right)\cdot(\sigma^{\prime\prime}_{i,j},x^{\prime\prime}_{i,j})}\text{.}

For an equivalence relation
R
on the carrier
X
of
\alpha
, we write
\equiv_{R}
for its
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma_{\tau}\times{I\mspace{-1mu}d})
-extension
i
.
e
. the equivalence relation on
\mathcal{D}(\Sigma_{\tau}\times X)
defined as follows:

\displaystyle\psi\equiv_{R}\phi\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\forall C\in X/R,\ \forall\sigma\in\Sigma_{\tau}\ \sum_{x\in C}\psi(\sigma,x)=\sum_{x\in C}\phi(\sigma,x)\text{.}

Definition 7.7
.

For a timed Segala system
\alpha
and an equivalence relation
R
on its carrier:

•

R
is a
(strong) timed bisimulation
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}x^{\prime}
and
x\xrightarrow{t}\psi
implies that there are
x^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t}\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi\equiv_{R}\psi^{\prime}
;

•

R
is a
(strong) time-abstract bisimulation
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}x^{\prime}
and
x\xrightarrow{t}\psi
implies that there are
x^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}\xrightarrow{t^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi\equiv_{R}\psi^{\prime}
;

•

R
is a
weak timed bisimulation
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}x^{\prime}
and
x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\psi
implies that there are
x^{\prime}
and
\psi^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi\equiv_{R}\psi^{\prime}
;

•

R
is a
weak time-abstract bisimulation
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}x^{\prime}
and
x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\psi
implies that there are
x^{\prime}
and
t^{\prime}
such that
x^{\prime}{\mathrel{\to}^{t^{\prime}}}\psi^{\prime}
and
\psi\equiv_{R}\psi^{\prime}
;

Timed Segala systems are modelled in the lax functors framework by putting
K
to be
\textnormal{Kl}({{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma})
. Notions of behavioural equivalence for TSSs defined above are all instances of
q
-bisimulation where behavioural morphisms are sourced from
Set
.

Proposition 7.8
.

For a timed Segala system
\alpha
with functor model
\underline{\alpha}
and an equivalence relation
R
on the carrier of
\alpha
:

(1)

R
is a (strong) timed bisimulation for
\alpha
iff it is a
id_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(2)

R
is a (strong) time-abstract bisimulation for
\alpha
iff it is a
!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(3)

R
is a weak timed bisimulation for
\alpha
iff it is a
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{T}}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(4)

R
is a weak time-abstract bisimulation for
\alpha
iff it is a
!_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
;

7.2.3.
Language equivalences

A (timed) language accepted by a (timed) Segala system is a convex set of distributions over (timed) words.
Let
\alpha
be a timed Segala system with accepting moves and let
X
be its state space. Formally, the timed/untimed languages strongly/weakly accepted by a state
x
of
\alpha
are the convex sets defined below.

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}\cdot w_{i}\in\mathrm{tl}_{\alpha}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\forall i\ \exists a_{0},\dots,a_{p},u_{0},\dots,u_{p}\text{ s.t. }\textstyle\sum a_{j}=1\text{, }\textstyle\sum a_{j}u_{j}=r_{i}\text{, and}

\displaystyle\text{if }w_{i}=t\text{ then }\forall j\leq p\,\exists\phi\in{{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}(X)\text{ s.t. }x\xrightarrow{t}u_{j}\cdot\checkmark+\phi

\displaystyle\text{if }w_{i}=t\sigma w^{\prime}\text{ then }\forall j\leq p\,\exists\phi,y_{0},\dots,y_{q},\psi_{0},\dots,\psi_{q}\text{ s.t. }

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle\forall k\leq q\ \psi_{k}\in\mathrm{tl}_{\alpha}(y_{k})\text{, }\forall z\notin\{y_{0},\dots,y_{q}\}\ \phi(\sigma,z)=0\text{, }

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle u_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{q}\phi(\sigma,y_{k})\psi_{k}(w^{\prime})\text{, and }x\xrightarrow{t}\phi

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}\cdot w_{i}\in\mathrm{utl}_{\alpha}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\forall i\ \exists t_{0}\sigma_{1}t_{1}\dots\sigma_{n}t_{n}\in\mathrm{tl}_{\alpha}(x)\text{ s.t. }w_{i}=\sigma_{1}\dots\sigma_{n}

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}\cdot w_{i}\in\mathrm{wtl}_{\alpha}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\forall i\ \exists a_{0},\dots,a_{p},u_{0},\dots,u_{p}\text{ s.t. }\textstyle\sum a_{j}=1\text{, }\textstyle\sum a_{j}u_{j}=r_{i}\text{, and}

\displaystyle\text{if }w_{i}=t\text{ then }\forall j\leq p\,\exists\phi\in{{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}(X)\text{ s.t. }x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}u_{j}\cdot\checkmark+\phi

\displaystyle\text{if }w_{i}=t\sigma w^{\prime}\text{ then }\forall j\leq p\,\exists\phi,y_{0},\dots,y_{q},\psi_{0},\dots,\psi_{q}\text{ s.t. }

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle\forall k\leq q\ \psi_{k}\in\mathrm{tl}_{\alpha}(y_{k})\text{, }\forall z\notin\{y_{0},\dots,y_{q}\}\ \phi(\sigma,z)=0\text{, }

\displaystyle\qquad\textstyle u_{j}=\sum_{k=0}^{q}\phi(\sigma,y_{k})\psi_{k}(w^{\prime})\text{, and }x{\mathrel{\to}^{t}}\phi

\displaystyle\textstyle\sum_{i=1}^{m}r_{i}\cdot w_{i}\in\mathrm{utl}_{\alpha}(x)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}{}

\displaystyle\forall i\ \exists t_{0}\sigma_{1}t_{1}\dots\sigma_{n}t_{n}\in\mathrm{wtl}_{\alpha}(x)\text{ s.t. }w_{i}=\sigma_{1}\dots\sigma_{n}

Definition 7.9
.

For a timed Segala system
\alpha
and an equivalence relation
R
on its carrier:

•

R
is a
timed language equivalence
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}y
implies that
\mathrm{tl}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathrm{tl}_{\alpha}(y)
;

•

R
is a
time-abstract language equivalence
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}y
implies that
\mathrm{utl}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathrm{utl}_{\alpha}(y)
;

•

R
is a
weak timed language equivalence
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}y
implies that
\mathrm{wtl}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathrm{wtl}_{\alpha}(y)
;

•

R
is a
weak time-abstract language equivalence
for
\alpha
if
x\mathrel{R}y
implies that
\mathrm{wutl}_{\alpha}(x)=\mathrm{wutl}_{\alpha}(y)
.

Timed Segala systems with accepting moves are modelled in the lax functors framework by putting
K
to be
\textnormal{Kl}({{\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark})
. Notions of language equivalence for TSSs defined above are all instances of
q
-bisimulation where behavioural morphisms are sourced from
\textnormal{Kl}({\mathcal{C\mspace{-2mu}M}})
.

Proposition 7.10
.

For a timed Segala system
\alpha
with functor model
\underline{\alpha}
and an equivalence relation
R
on the carrier of
\alpha
:

(1)

R
is a timed language equivalence for
\alpha
iff it is a
id_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-language equivalence for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(2)

R
is a time-abstract language equivalence for
\alpha
iff it is a
!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}
-language equivalence for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(3)

R
is a weak timed language equivalence for
\alpha
iff it is a
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{T}}
-language equivalence for
\underline{\alpha}
;

(4)

R
is a weak time-abstract language equivalence for
\alpha
iff it is a
!_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-language equivalence for
\underline{\alpha}
;

It follows from
Propositions

7.8
,
7.10
,
4.12
and
4.14
that notions of bisimulation and language equivalence for TSSs given in
Definitions

7.7
and
7.9
are organised in the spectrum shown in
Figure

1
.

7.3.
Fully probabilistic processes and beyond

The aim of this subsection is to show how probabilistic systems (or more generally, weighted systems
[
10
]
) fit into the framework presented in this paper. Weighted systems, unlike other systems presented in this section tend not to satisfy one of the main assumptions of Theorem
4.2
, namely, left distributivity. There are two (essentially equivalent) workarounds to this problem known in the literature. One is to consider the continuous continuation monad from
[
12
]
which is a natural extension of the weighted systems type monad and whose Kleisli category satisfies left distributivity (called
algebraicity
in
[
12
]
). The idea behind the second solution, which is given in
[
10
]
, is similar and formulated on the categorical level: given a suitably order enriched category
K
which does not satisfy left distributivity we can embed it into
\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
, a category with left distributivity (which admits saturation). Although, the latter proposal seems to be more general than the former, the first solution preserves the coalgebraic nature of systems,
i
.
e
. systems of a type
X\to TX
are extended to systems of a type
X\to T^{\prime}X
, where
T^{\prime}
is the new, richer monad. The second embedding, if applied to
\textnormal{K}=\textnormal{Kl}(T)
does not necessarily yield a category
\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
which is a Kleisli category for some monad
T^{\prime}
. However, as shown in
[
10
]
, if we adopt the general embedding
\textnormal{K}\hookrightarrow\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
approach for saturation admittance, weak behavioural equivalence for systems in
K
(as defined in
loc
.
cit
.) remains the same regardless of the choice of the embedding. In other words, it is enough to embed
K
into
a
left distributive category (with saturation) and not into any particular choice of the left distributive category
5
5

5

This also means that if
K
is left distributive and satisfies all other required properties then we can embed it into itself. Hence, using the guidelines of
[
10
]
, it can be easily shown that the setting we present below in
Section

7.3.1
generalizes the one from
Section

4
.
. See
[
10
]
for a discussion.

We will recall some of the ingredients of the construction of a left distributive category
\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
from
[
10
]
and elaborate more on how can this be used in order to define
q
-saturations and
q
-behavioural morphisms. We start off with a general construction and then, in the next paragraph, we instantiate it on probabilistic processes.

7.3.1.
The embedding

The original construction of
\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
was presented in the context of
\omega\textnormal{-Cpo}^{\!\vee\!}
-enriched categories. By
\omega\textnormal{-Cpo}^{\!\vee\!}
we denote the category of posets which admit binary joins and countable joins
\bigvee_{n<\omega}x_{n}
of
\omega
-chains
x_{1}\leq x_{2}\leq\ldots
with morphisms preserving joins of
\omega
-chains. Hence, a category is
\omega\textnormal{-Cpo}^{\!\vee\!}
-enriched if each hom-set is a poset which admits binary joins and joins of
\omega
-chains, and the composition preserves the latter. Let
K
be a small
6
6

6

Smallness of
K
is required to guarantee
\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
is locally small
[
10
]
. However, our aim is to apply this approach for
\textnormal{K}=\mathcal{K}l(T)
where
T
is a monad for weighted systems. Unfortunately, it is not a
small
category. The simplest solution in this case is to take
K
as a suitable full subcategory of

\mathcal{K}l(T)
that meets our requirements. For example, if we are
interested in
T
-coalgebras whose base category is
Set
and
which have a carrier of cardinality below
\kappa
then we can take

K
to have exactly one set of cardinality
\lambda
for any
\lambda<\kappa
.
In particular, if
\kappa=\omega
then
K
is the dual category to the
Lawvere theory for
T
(see
e
.
g
.
[
16
]
). See
[
10
]
for a thorough discussion.

\omega\textnormal{-Cpo}^{\!\vee\!}
-enriched category. Following
[
10
]
we define
\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
to be the opposite of the category
[\textnormal{K},\omega\textnormal{-Cpo}^{\!\vee\!}]^{\omega\textnormal{-Cpo}^{\!\vee\!}}
of lax functors and oplax natural transformations between them. Taking any object
X
to its representable functor
\widehat{X}\triangleq\textnormal{K}(X,-)
and any morphism
f\colon X\to Y
to
\widehat{f}\triangleq\textnormal{K}(f,-)
defines the embedding
\widehat{(-)}\colon\textnormal{K}\to\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
into an
\omega\textnormal{-Cpo}^{\!\vee\!}
-enriched category which is left distributive
[
10
]
. If, in the above, we replace
\omega\textnormal{-Cpo}^{\!\vee\!}
with
\textnormal{DCpo}^{\!\vee\!}
,
i
.
e
. the category of complete orders which admit binary joins with directed suprema preserved by the morphisms, then we also get a true statement (
conf. loc. cit.
). In this case, as noted in the footnote under Theorem
4.2
, the category
\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
satisfies the assumptions of this theorem.

Here, we need to point out an important property of the embedding
\widehat{(-)}\colon\textnormal{K}\to\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
above. Namely, for fixed objects
X,Y\in\textnormal{K}
the hom-poset restriction
\widehat{(-)}\colon\textnormal{K}(X,Y)\to\widehat{\textnormal{K}}(\widehat{X},\widehat{Y})
of
\widehat{(-)}\colon\textnormal{K}\to\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
admits a left order adjoint
\Theta\colon\widehat{\textnormal{K}}(\widehat{X},\widehat{Y})\to\textnormal{K}({X},{Y})
which is given by
[
10
]

\Theta(\phi:\widehat{X}\to\widehat{Y}\in\widehat{\textnormal{K}})=\Theta(\phi:\textnormal{K}(Y,-)\to\textnormal{K}(X,-))\triangleq\phi_{Y}(id_{Y})\text{.}

We are now ready to define
q
-bisimulations for
\pi\in[\textnormal{M},\textnormal{K}]^{J}
.

7.3.2.
q
-saturations and
q
-bisimulations

Assume
K
is
\textnormal{DCpo}^{\!\vee\!}
-enriched and
J
is a subcategory of
K
. Take
q\colon M\to N
an onto monoid homomorphism, and consider
\widehat{J}\colon\textnormal{J}\to\textnormal{K}\to\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
. By Theorem
4.2
and the above remarks,
\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
admits
q
-saturation w.r.t.
\widehat{J}
:

[\textnormal{M},\widehat{\textnormal{K}}]^{\widehat{J}}
[\textnormal{N},\widehat{\textnormal{K}}]^{\widehat{J}}
\Sigma_{q}^{\widehat{J}}
[q,\widehat{\textnormal{K}}]^{\widehat{J}}
\dashv

Assume
\pi\in[\textnormal{M},\textnormal{K}]^{J}
. A
q
-behavioural morphism
on
\pi
is any morphism
f
in
J
with domain
\Sigma_{q}^{\widehat{J}}(\widehat{\pi})(\ast)
such that there is
\pi^{\prime}_{n}\in[\textnormal{N},\textnormal{K}]^{J}
making

\Theta\left(\widehat{J(f)}\circ\Sigma_{q}^{\widehat{J}}(\widehat{\pi})_{n}\right)=\Theta(\widehat{\pi^{\prime}}_{n}\circ\widehat{J(f)})=\pi_{n}^{\prime}\circ J(f).

(5)

A
q
-bisimulation
on
\pi
is a kernel pair of a
q
-behavioural morphism whose domain is
\pi
.

This generalization goes along the lines of the generalization of weak behavioural equivalence via saturation proposed by us in
[
10
]
. The remaining part of this subsection will focus on instantiating it on Markov chains.

7.3.3.
Example: Markov chains

At first, we present basic categorical ingredients needed to model Markov chains in the lax functorial setting.
Consider the
Set
endofunctor
\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]}
given on any set
X
and on any function
f\colon X\to Y
as
follows:

\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]}X\triangleq\{\phi\colon X\to{[0,\infty]}\mid|\{x\mid\phi(x)\neq 0\}|\leq\omega\}\qquad\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]}f(\phi)(y)\triangleq\sum_{x\in f^{-1}(y)\}}\phi(x)\text{.}

This functor extends to a monad whose multiplication

\mu
and unit
\eta
are given on their components by:

\mu_{X}(\phi)(x)\triangleq\sum_{\psi\in\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]}X}\phi(\psi)\cdot\psi(x)\qquad\eta_{X}(x)(x^{\prime})\triangleq\begin{cases}1&\text{ if $x=x^{\prime}$}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}

For any
X,Y\in\textnormal{Set}
and
f,g\in\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})(X,Y)
define:

f\leq g\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\triangle}}{{\iff}}f(x)(y)\leq g(x)(y)\text{ for any }x\in X,\ y\in Y.

As pointed out in
[
10
]
the category
\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})
is
\omega\textnormal{-Cpo}^{\!\vee\!}
-enriched. It is
not
left distributive but it is
Set
-right distributive w.r.t. countable suprema
[
10
]
. The category
\widehat{\textnormal{K}}
we embed (a full subcategory of)
\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})
into by following the guidelines of
[
10
]
admits countable suprema, is left distributive and
Set
-right distributive w.r.t. them. However, it does not admit
arbitrary
non-empty suprema. Yet, if we carefully analyse the proof of Theorem
4.2
then it turns out that the above assumptions suffice for
!_{\mathbb{N}}\colon\mathbb{N}\to 1
-saturation
\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{N}}}:[\mathbb{N},\widehat{\textnormal{K}}]^{\textnormal{Set}}\to[1,\widehat{\textnormal{K}}]^{\textnormal{Set}}
to exist
7
7

7

Indeed, in this case only countable suprema are considered in the definition of
\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{N}}}
.
.

Consider any ordinary functor
\pi\colon\mathbb{N}\to\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})
in
[\mathbb{N},\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})]^{\textnormal{Set}}
and a
Set
-map
f
whose domain is
\pi(\ast)
. We have the following statement (see also
[
6
]
).

Lemma 7.11
.

\Theta(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{N}}}(\widehat{\pi}))=\mu x.(f^{\sharp}\vee x\circ\pi_{1}),

(6)

with the least fixpoint calculated in
\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})
and
(-)^{\sharp}:\textnormal{Set}\to\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})
the inclusion functor.

Proof.

Put
\Pi=\bigvee_{n<\omega}\widehat{\pi}_{n}
. By Theorem
4.2
we have:

\displaystyle(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{N}}}(\widehat{\pi}))=\Theta(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ\bigvee_{l}(\Pi)^{l})=\Theta(\bigvee_{l}\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ(\Pi)^{l})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\dagger}}{{=}}\bigvee_{l}\Theta(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ(\Pi)^{l})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\diamond}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\bigvee_{l}G^{l}(f^{\sharp})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\Box}}{{=}}\mu x.(f^{\sharp}\vee x\circ\pi_{1}).

The equation
(\dagger)
follows from the fact that
\Theta
preserves arbitrary suprema (as it is a left adjoint). If we put
G(x)=f^{\sharp}\vee\bigvee_{n}x\circ\pi_{n}
then
(\diamond)
holds, which follows by induction. Indeed, for
l=0
we vacuously have:
G^{0}(f^{\sharp})=\Theta(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ(\Pi)^{0})
. Assume the identity
G^{l}(f^{\sharp})=\Theta(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ(\Pi)^{l})
holds for
l
and consider:

\displaystyle\Theta(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ(\Pi)^{l+1})=\Theta(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ(\Pi)^{l}\circ\Pi)=\Theta(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ(\Pi)^{l}\circ\bigvee_{n<\omega}\widehat{\pi_{n}})=\bigvee_{n<\omega}\Theta(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ(\Pi)^{l}\circ\widehat{\pi_{n}})=

\displaystyle\bigvee_{n<\omega}\Theta(\widehat{f^{\sharp}}\circ(\Pi)^{l})\circ{\pi_{n}}=\bigvee_{n}G^{l}(f^{\sharp})\circ\pi_{n}=f^{\sharp}\vee\bigvee_{n}G^{l}(f^{\sharp})\circ\pi_{n}=G^{l+1}(f^{\sharp}).

Finally, in order to see
(\Box)
holds consider
H(x)=f^{\sharp}\vee x\circ\pi_{1}
. Obviously
H(x)\leq G(x)
. However, since
\pi
is an ordinary functor, we also have:

G(x)=f^{\sharp}\vee\bigvee_{n}x\circ(\pi_{1})^{n}\text{.}

Hence, if we take
G_{m}(x)=f^{\sharp}\vee\bigvee_{n=1}^{m}x\circ(\pi_{1})^{n}
then by induction
G_{m}^{n}(f^{\sharp})\leq H^{m\cdot n}(f^{\sharp})
. Therefore,

\bigvee_{n,m}G^{n}_{m}(f^{\sharp})\leq\bigvee_{n}H^{n}(f^{\sharp})\leq\bigvee_{n}G^{n}(f^{\sharp})\text{.}

Since
\bigvee_{n}H^{n}(f^{\sharp})=\mu x.(f^{\sharp}\vee x\circ\pi_{1})
, as our category is
\omega\textnormal{-Cpo}^{\!\vee\!}
-enriched, this proves the assertion.
∎

Hence, the identity (
5
) becomes:

\mu x.(f^{\sharp}\vee x\circ\pi_{1})=\pi^{\prime}_{1}\circ f^{\sharp},

where the least fix point and the composition are calculated in
\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})
.

We are ready to elaborate more on a Markov chain example. We will now recall some notions from Markov chain theory. The reader is referred to
e
.
g
.
[
35
]
for basic definitions and properties.

Let
(X_{n})_{n\leq\omega}
be an Markov chain (or MC in short). We call the chain
(X_{n})

homogeneous
whenever

\mathbb{P}(X_{n}=j\mid X_{m}=i)=\mathbb{P}(X_{n-m}=j\mid X_{0}=i)
.
Any homogeneous MC
(X_{n})
on a finite state space
S
gives rise to its
transition matrix family
,
i
.
e
. a family
\{P(n):S^{2}\to[0,1]\}_{n\geq 0}
whose
ij
-th entry
p_{ij}(n)=P(n)(i,j)
describes the conditional
transition probabilities
:

p_{ij}(n)=\mathbb{P}(X_{n}=j\mid X_{0}=i)\text{.}

The transition matrix family satisfies
P(0)=I
and
P(m+n)=P(m)\cdot P(n)
, where
I
is the identity matrix and
\cdot
is the matrix multiplication. Hence,
P(n)=P(1)^{n}
. The family
\{P(n)=P(1)^{n}\}_{n<\omega}
yields an assignment
\pi:\mathbb{N}\to\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})
given for any
n\in\mathbb{N}
by:

\pi(\ast)=S,\quad\pi_{n}\colon S\to\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]}S;\pi_{n}(i)(j)=p_{ij}(n)\text{.}

The assignment
\pi=(\pi_{n})
is an ordinary functor
\mathbb{N}\to\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})
and, hence, is a member of
[\mathbb{N},\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]})]^{\textnormal{Set}}
and will be referred to as the
transition functor
of the chain
(X_{n})
.

Consider an equivalence relation
R
on the state space
S
. For an abstract class
C
of
R
let us denote:

p_{i,C}^{n}=\mathbb{P}(X_{m}\in C\text{ for some }m\geq n\mid X_{0}=i)\text{ and }p_{i,C}=p_{i,C}^{0}.

Lemma 7.12
.

The family
\{p_{i,C}\}_{i\in S}
satisfies:

\displaystyle p_{i,C}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{cc}1&\text{ if }i\in C,\\
\sup_{n\geq 0}\sum_{j\in S}p_{j,C}\cdot p_{i,j}(n)&\text{ otherwise.}\end{array}\right.

Proof.

It is clear that if
i\in C
then
p_{i,C}=1
. For
i\notin C
we have:

\displaystyle p_{i,C}=\sup_{n\geq 0}p_{i,C}^{n}=\sup_{n\geq 0}\sum_{j\in S}\mathbb{P}(X_{m}\in C,m\geq n\mid X_{n}=j,X_{0}=i)\cdot\mathbb{P}(X_{n}=j\mid X_{0}=i)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\dagger}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 0}\sum_{j\in S}\mathbb{P}(X_{m}\in C,m\geq n\mid X_{n}=j)\cdot\mathbb{P}(X_{n}=j\mid X_{0}=i)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\dagger\dagger}}{{=}}

\displaystyle\sup_{n\geq 0}\sum_{j\in S}\mathbb{P}(X_{m}\in C,m\geq 0\mid X_{0}=j)\cdot\mathbb{P}(X_{n}=j\mid X_{0}=i)=\sup_{n\geq 0}\sum_{j\in S}p_{j,C}\cdot p_{i,j}(n).

The identity
(\dagger)
follows by
(X_{n})
being markovian and
(\dagger\dagger)
by homogeneity of the given process.
∎

Theorem 7.13
.

The relation
R\subseteq S\times S
is a
!\colon\mathbb{N}\to 1
-bisimluation on the transition functor
\pi
of a homogeneous MC
(X_{n})_{t\geq 0}
provided that for any
(i,j)\in R
and any abstract class
C
of
R
we have:

\mathbb{P}(X_{n}\in C\text{ for some }n\geq 0\mid X_{0}=i)=\mathbb{P}(X_{n}\in C\text{ for some }n\geq 0\mid X_{0}=j).

(9)

Proof.

Let
f\colon S\to S_{/R};i\mapsto[i]_{/R}
. By
Lemma

7.12
and (
6
) we have:
\mu x.(f^{\sharp}\vee x\circ\pi_{1})(i)(C)=p_{i,C}
. Satisfaction of the identity (
9
) is equivalent to existence of a map
\beta\colon S_{/R}\to\mathcal{F}_{[0,\infty]}S_{/R}
which makes
\mu x.(f^{\sharp}\vee x\circ\pi_{1})=\beta\circ f^{\sharp}
hold. This proves the assertion.
∎

8.
Conclusion

In this paper we introduced a general definition of behavioural models with explicit time flow and their behavioural theory. The framework is based on (lax) functors over order-enriched categories, typically Kleisli categories. This approach allows us to encode in the index category how computations are observed (
e
.
g
. if time durations are associated to single steps or entire computations) while abstracting from other computational aspects which are modelled in the base category.
A key advantage of this separation is that we can fit into our setting many models of interest using Kleisli categories for standard monads like: the powerset monad, the quantalic monad, the convex set monad, the generalised multiset monad, and many more (see
[
10
,
7
]
for more examples of compatible monads).

Although the categories induced by these monads do not necessarily satisfy our main assumptions, namely left distributivity, our framework is still applicable: we have shown that one only needs an embedding into category a category that satisfies our main assumptions.
As an example we applied this approach to weighted transition systems thus covering fully probabilistic systems and discrete Markov chains, among others.
This technique builds on our previous work on saturation and weak bisimulation: in
[
10
]
we provided a general method for constructing such embeddings and identified the class embeddings compatible with saturation.
Notably, these results apply also to categories different from
Set
; in
loc
.
cit
. we have also considered presheaves, compact Hausdorff spaces and measurable spaces.

Our results are built on lax functors and the theory of saturation which we introduced and developed in a series of works
[
6
,
10
,
7
]
that provided a general coalgebraic characterisation of weak behavioural equivalences covering many types of systems of interest.
In this paper (and its conference version,
[
9
]
) we extended the theory of saturation and saturation-based behavioural equivalence developing the notion of
general saturation
for lax functors on a monoid category and
q
-behavioural equivalence
.

Our framework provides a rich behavioural theory that encompasses wide range of behavioural equivalences found in the literature: we have shown that timed bisimulation, timed language equivalence, as well as their weak and time-abstract counterparts, are all instances of
q
-behavioural equivalence.
Moreover, we proved that all these notions of equivalences are naturally organised by their discriminating power to form a spectrum (
Figure

1
) and that this result does not depend on the type of the systems under scrutiny.

This is not the first work to consider timed behavioural models and their behavioural theory from a categorical perspective.
In
[
13
]
,
Gribovskaya and Virbitskaite 2010
present a categorical view of timed weak bisimulation. Their approach is based on open-maps bisimulation and is limited to non-deterministic timed transition systems.
In
[
20
]
,
Kick 2003
presents a coalgebraic framework for behavioural models that combine timed transitions with discrete ones: time-dependent computations are modelled by a suitable comonad over
Set
and then combined with other behavioural aspects by means of comonad products.
The approach is inherently limited to systems that can be modelled in
Set
, that distinguish between timed and discrete actions (thus excluding timed automata and timed CSP), and to strong timed bisimulation.
The last observation is the main distinguishing point between
loc
.
cit
. and this work: because of technical difficulties associated with comonad products, this approach appears less flexible then ours when behavioural equivalences beside strong timed bisimulation are considered.

The categorical characterization of timed behavioural models paves the way for further interesting lines of research.
One line is to extend our framework to support other index categories besides monoids. This would allow us to study more structured state spaces and computations like
e
.
g
. those found in alternating games.
Another line is to extend the framework with results from the rich theory of coalgebras such as
\omega
-behaviours
[
8
]
, minimization
[
3
]
, determinisation
[
43
]
, and up-to techniques
[
4
]
.

Appendix A
Omitted proofs

\proofatend@proofof

.

Let
\alpha
be a TTS.
Assume
R\subseteq X^{2}
is a timed bisimulation for
\alpha
and let
f\colon X\to Y\cong X/R
be the canonical projection in
Set
.
Let
\underline{\beta}\colon\mathbb{T}^{\ast}\to\textnormal{K}
be the lax functor defined by the family
\{\beta_{t}\colon Y\to\mathcal{P}(\Sigma_{\tau}\times Y)\}_{t\in\mathbb{T}}
where:

\beta_{t}(y)\triangleq\{(\sigma,y^{\prime})\mid\forall x\in f^{-1}(y)\,\exists x^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(y^{\prime})\text{ s.t. }x\xrightarrow{(\sigma,t)}x^{\prime}\}\text{.}

From the definition of
\beta_{t}
it is immediate to check that for any
t\in\mathbb{T}
:

(\sigma,f(x^{\prime}))\in(\underline{\beta}_{t}\circ f)(x)\iff(\sigma,x^{\prime})\in(\underline{\alpha}_{t})(x)

and hence that
f\circ\Sigma_{id_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}}(\underline{\alpha})=\underline{\beta}\circ f
. Therefore, the quotient map
f
induced by
R
extends to a
id_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-behavioural morphism for
\underline{\alpha}
.

For the converse implication take
R
to be a
id_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
. This means that there is a lax functor
\beta\in[\mathbb{T}^{\ast},\textnormal{K}]
whose carrier
Y
is given by the set of abstract classes of
R
and such that the quotient map
f\colon X\to Y
induced by
R
satisfies:

f\circ\Sigma_{id_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}}(\underline{\alpha})=\underline{\beta}\circ f\text{.}

In particular, for any
(\sigma,t)\in\Sigma_{\tau}\times\mathbb{T}
we have that
(\sigma,f(x^{\prime}))\in(\underline{\beta}_{t}\circ f)(x)\iff(\sigma,x^{\prime})\in(\underline{\alpha}_{t})(x)
from which it immediately follows that
R
is a timed bisimulation for
\alpha
.∎

\proofatend@proofof

.

Let
\alpha
be a TTS.
Assume
R\subseteq X^{2}
is a weak timed bisimulation for
\alpha
and let
f\colon X\to Y\cong X/R
be the canonical projection in
Set
.
Let
\underline{\beta}\colon\mathbb{T}\to\textnormal{K}
be the lax functor defined on each
t\in\mathbb{T}
and
y\in Y
as:

\beta_{t}(y)\triangleq\bigcup_{t_{1}\dots t_{n}\in\varepsilon_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}(t)}\left\{(\sigma_{i},y^{\prime})\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}\forall x\in f^{-1}(y)\,\exists x^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(y^{\prime})\text{ such that }\\
x\xrightarrow{(\sigma_{1},t_{1})}\cdots\xrightarrow{(\sigma_{i},t_{i})}\cdots\xrightarrow{(\sigma_{n},t_{n})}x^{\prime}\text{ and }j\neq i\implies\sigma_{j}=\tau\end{array}\right\}\text{.}

From the definition of
\beta_{t}
it is immediate to check that for any
t\in\mathbb{T}
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\tau}
:

(\sigma,f(x^{\prime}))\in(\underline{\beta}_{t}\circ f)(x)\iff(\sigma,x^{\prime})\in\Sigma_{\varepsilon_{\mathbb{T}}}(\underline{\alpha})_{t}(x)

and hence that the quotient map
f
for
R
extends to a
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{T}}
-behavioural morphism for
\underline{\alpha}
.

For the converse implication take
R
to be a
\varepsilon_{\mathbb{T}}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
. This means that there is a lax functor
\beta\in[\mathbb{T},\textnormal{K}]
whose carrier
Y
is given by the set of abstract classes of
R
and such that the quotient map
f\colon X\to Y
induced by
R
satisfies:

f\circ\Sigma_{\varepsilon_{\mathbb{T}}}(\underline{\alpha})=\underline{\beta}\circ f\text{.}

In particular, for any
t\in\mathbb{T}
and
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\tau}
we have that
(\sigma,f(x^{\prime}))\in(\underline{\beta}_{t}\circ f)(x)
iff

(\sigma,x^{\prime})\in\bigvee_{\vec{t}\in\varepsilon_{\mathbb{T}}^{-1}(t)}\underline{\alpha}_{\vec{t}}(x)
. By straightforward definition expansion we conclude that
R
is a weak timed bisimulation for
\alpha
.∎

\proofatend@proofof

.

Assume
R\subseteq X^{2}
is a time-abstract bisimulation for
\alpha
and let
f\colon X\to Y\cong X/R
be the canonical projection in
Set
. Define
\beta\colon Y\to\mathcal{P}(\Sigma_{\tau}\times Y)
by:

\beta(y)=\{(\sigma,y^{\prime})\mid\forall x\in y\exists x^{\prime}\in y^{\prime}\text{ and }\exists t\text{ s.t. }(\sigma,t,x^{\prime})\in\alpha(x)\}\text{.}

Then the morphism
f\colon X\to Y
in
Set
which maps any element to its abstract class satisfies
f\circ\Sigma_{\sharp}(\underline{\alpha})_{1}=\beta\circ f
(here,
\circ
denotes the composition in
\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{P}^{\Sigma})
). Note that
\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}}(\alpha)_{n}=(\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}}(\alpha)_{1})^{n}
and put
\underline{\beta}\in[1^{\ast},\textnormal{K}]
to be
\underline{\beta}_{n}=\beta^{n}
. We have
f\circ\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}}(\underline{\alpha})_{n}=\underline{\beta}_{n}\circ f
. This proves that
R
is a
!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}
-bisimulation.

For the converse implication take
R
to be a
!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
. This means that there is a lax functor
\pi^{\prime}\in[\mathbb{N},\textnormal{K}]
whose carrier
Y
is given by the set of abstract classes of
R
for which the map
f\colon X\to Y
which assigns any
x
to its abstract class satisfies
f\circ\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}}(\underline{\alpha})_{n}=\pi^{\prime}_{n}\circ f\text{ for }n\in\mathbb{N}
.
In particular the above equality holds for
n=1
. By the construction of
\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{T}}^{\ast}}(\underline{\alpha})_{1}
it instantly follows that
R
is a time-abstract bisimulation for
\alpha
.∎

\proofatend@proofof

.

Assume
R\subseteq X^{2}
is a weak time-abstract bisimulation for
\alpha
and let
f\colon X\to Y\cong X/R
be the canonical projection in
Set
.
Let
\underline{\beta}\in\textnormal{K}
be the LTS given on each
y\in Y
as:

\beta(y)\triangleq\bigcup_{t_{1}\dots t_{n}\in\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}\left\{(\sigma_{i},y^{\prime})\,\middle|\,\begin{array}[]{l}\forall x\in f^{-1}(y)\,\exists x^{\prime}\in f^{-1}(y^{\prime})\text{ such that }\\
x\xrightarrow{(\sigma_{1},t_{1})}\cdots\xrightarrow{(\sigma_{i},t_{i})}\cdots\xrightarrow{(\sigma_{n},t_{n})}x^{\prime}\text{ and }j\neq i\implies\sigma_{j}=\tau\end{array}\right\}\text{.}

From the definition of
\beta
it is immediate to check that for any
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\tau}
:

(\sigma,f(x^{\prime}))\in(\beta\circ f)(x)\iff(\sigma,x^{\prime})\in\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}}(\underline{\alpha})(x)

and hence that the quotient map
f
for
R
extends to a
!_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-behavioural morphism for
\underline{\alpha}
.

For the converse implication take
R
to be a
!_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}
-bisimulation for
\underline{\alpha}
. This means that there is a lax functor
\beta\in[1,\textnormal{K}]
whose carrier
Y
is given by the set of abstract classes of
R
and such that the quotient map
f\colon X\to Y
induced by
R
satisfies:

f\circ\Sigma_{!_{\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}}(\underline{\alpha})=\underline{\beta}\circ f\text{.}

In particular, for any
\sigma\in\Sigma_{\tau}
we have that
(\sigma,f(x^{\prime}))\in(\underline{\beta}\circ f)(x)\iff(\sigma,x^{\prime})\in\bigvee_{\vec{t}\in\mathbb{T}^{\ast}}\underline{\alpha}_{\vec{t}}(x)
. By straightforward definition expansion we conclude that

is a weak time-abstract bisimulation for

.∎

\proofatend@proofof

.

Here, we will only prove the last assertion as the remaining ones are proved in an analogous manner (see also the proof of
Propositions

6.2
,
6.3
and
6.4
). Since we will work with lax functors
1\to\textnormal{K}=\textnormal{Kl}({\mathcal{P}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark})
we will simplify the notation and associate any such assignment
\pi\colon 1\to\textnormal{K}
with the endomorphism it is induced by
\pi_{0}\colon\pi(\ast)\to{\mathcal{P}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}(\pi(\ast))
which satisfies
id\leq\pi_{0}
and
\pi_{0}\circ\pi_{0}\leq\pi_{0}
.

For the functor
\underline{\alpha}\colon\mathbb{T}^{\ast}\to\textnormal{K}
the lax functor

\Sigma_{!}(\underline{\alpha})\colon 1\to\textnormal{K}=\textnormal{Kl}({\mathcal{P}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark})
is given in terms of a single endomorphism
\Sigma_{!}(\underline{\alpha})\colon X\to{\mathcal{P}}^{\Sigma}_{\scriptstyle\checkmark}X
by:

\displaystyle x\xrightarrow{\sigma}_{\Sigma_{!}(\underline{\alpha})}y\text{ whenever }x{\mathrel{\to}^{(\sigma,t)}}_{\alpha}y\text{ and }x\downarrow\text{ whenever }x{\mathrel{\to}^{(\tau,t)}}_{\alpha}y\text{ and }y\downarrow\text{ for some }t\in\mathbb{T}.

A morphism
f\colon X\to\mathcal{P}Y
is a weak behavioural morphism for
\alpha
whenever there is a lax functor
\underline{\beta}\colon 1\to\textnormal{K}
whose carrier is
Y
which makes:

f^{\sharp}\circ\Sigma_{!}(\underline{\alpha})=\underline{\beta}\circ f^{\sharp}
. This equation turns
f^{\sharp}
into a coalgebra homomorphism with the base category
\textnormal{Kl}(\mathcal{P})
. By applying the guidelines of
[
7
, Example 7.3]
we get the desired conclusion.∎
